{"parentUuid":"21bda928-0a9f-4cb4-b712-dc573dc6d53c","isSidechain":true,"agentId":"a890b3cad31fe1473","message":{"model":"claude-opus-5","id":"msg_011CdWJ3Hp7bPohu1wTUwET2","type":"message","role":"assistant","content":[{"type":"text","text":"I have everything I need. Here is the trace report.\n\n---\n\n# T10 — Session minting from a license key + magic-link path\n\n## Finding 1 — Forced login (login CSRF): a link signs the victim into the **attacker's** account\n\n**trace**\n1. `nextjs/app/[locale]/credits/page.tsx:17` — public server component, no session required\n2. `credits/page.tsx:28-31` — `raw.startsWith(\"HW-\")` → `redirect(\"/${locale}/user/sign-in?licenseKey=...&returnTo=...\")`\n3. `nextjs/middleware.ts:130` (`isUserSignIn` branch) — logged-out victim has no cookie → falls through to `intlMiddleware`, page renders\n4. `nextjs/app/[locale]/user/sign-in/page.tsx:49-54` — `useEffect(..., [])` auto-fires `handleLicenseKeySignInWithKey(licenseKeyParam)` **on mount, with no user interaction and no confirmation UI** (only a \"Signing you in…\" spinner, page.tsx:139-146)\n5. `sign-in/page.tsx:58-63` — **same-origin** `fetch(\"/api/auth/sign-in/license-key\")`\n6. `node_modules/better-auth/dist/api/index.mjs:157-160` — `originCheckMiddleware` is a global `path: \"/**\"` router middleware, so it *does* run; but `validateOrigin` (`api/middlewares/origin-check.mjs:100-118`) only compares the `Origin` header to `trustedOrigins`, which defaults to `[new URL(baseURL).origin]` (`context/helpers.mjs:60-75`). The fetch is same-origin, so `Origin` == the product's own origin → **passes**\n7. `nextjs/src/lib/auth-license-key-plugin.ts:63` — `internalAdapter.createSession(foundUser.id)`; `:74` `setSessionCookie`\n\n**seam** — EP-55 (`credits/page.tsx`) ↔ EP-56 (`sign-in/page.tsx`). The credits page treats `?license_key=` as a *convenience deep-link hint from the desktop app*; the sign-in page treats the same value as a *fully-trusted bearer credential worth an unattended session mint*. One layer assumed a low-value routing hint; the next made it a high-value credential.\n\nCritically, better-auth's two anti-CSRF mechanisms are both structurally inapplicable here:\n- `formCsrfMiddleware` (the `Sec-Fetch-Site: cross-site && Sec-Fetch-Mode: navigate` block, `origin-check.mjs:126-150`) is opted into per-endpoint by `sign-in.mjs:146` and `sign-up.mjs:24` only. **The license-key plugin's `createAuthEndpoint` does not include it** (`auth-license-key-plugin.ts:16-23` has no `use: [...]`).\n- Even if it did, the attack is a *navigation* to the app's own page followed by a *same-origin XHR*, so `Sec-Fetch-Site` is `same-origin`. `trustedOrigins` being unset changes nothing — adding it would not help.\n\n**trigger_sketch**\n```\nhttps://hyperwhisper.com/en/credits?license_key=HW-AAAA-BBBB-CCCC-DDDD\n```\n(attacker's own key; `?id=` works identically). Sent by email/DM/QR/ad. No POST, no form, no JS on the attacker's side.\n\n**consequence** — Victim silently lands on `/en/user/dashboard` holding a 7-day session cookie (`sessionConfig.expiresIn` default `3600*24*7`, `create-context.mjs:141`) for the attacker's user. Then:\n- Victim buys credits from that dashboard → `app/api/checkout/credits/route.ts:88-121` resolves the wallet from `normalizedLicenseKey`, so **the money lands in the attacker's wallet** and the Stripe customer is the attacker's.\n- `app/api/customer/profile/route.ts:44-52` returns `key: license.key` **in plaintext** for every license on the account — so anything the victim screenshots/shares from that page is the attacker's key, and conversely the victim believes it is theirs.\n- If the victim then uses the Email tab to \"add\" their address, the attacker's account harvests it.\n- The victim's own real key never gets used, so the failure is invisible until they check their balance.\n\n**confidence** — high\n\n---\n\n## Finding 2 — `sanitizeReturnTo` bypass via tab/CR/LF → open redirect in `middleware.ts`\n\n**trace**\n1. `nextjs/src/lib/license-key-redirect.ts:16-19` — accepts anything matching `startsWith(\"/\") && !startsWith(\"//\") && !startsWith(\"/\\\\\")`\n2. `nextjs/middleware.ts:130-137` — already-authenticated visitor to the sign-in page: `const redirectUrl = sanitizeReturnTo(returnTo, ...)` then `NextResponse.redirect(new URL(redirectUrl, request.url))`\n\n**seam** — `license-key-redirect.ts` ↔ `middleware.ts:135`. The sanitizer's contract is *\"string-prefix inspection is enough to prove same-origin\"*. `new URL()` is a WHATWG parser that **strips ASCII tab, LF and CR from the input before parsing**, so a string that fails all three prefix checks in `String` space can still parse as protocol-relative. Verified:\n\n```\n\"/\\t/evil.com\"   -> sanitizer PASSES -> new URL(...) => https://evil.com/\n\"/\\n/evil.com\"   -> sanitizer PASSES -> new URL(...) => https://evil.com/\n\"/\\r/evil.com\"   -> sanitizer PASSES -> new URL(...) => https://evil.com/\n\"/\\t\\\\evil.com\"  -> sanitizer PASSES -> new URL(...) => https://evil.com/\n\"/%2f%2fevil.com\"-> sanitizer PASSES -> stays same-origin (not a bypass)\n```\n\nThe test that **pins the wrong behaviour** is `tests/license-key-redirect.test.ts:60` — `\"sanitizeReturnTo blocks protocol-relative and backslash open redirects\"` — it asserts exactly `//evil.com`, `/\\evil.com`, `https://evil.com` and nothing else, which is precisely the three string-prefix cases the implementation was written against. The doc comment at `license-key-redirect.ts:3-8` states the same closed-world assumption.\n\n**trigger_sketch** (victim must be signed in; `searchParams.get()` percent-decodes, so `%09` arrives as a real tab)\n```\nhttps://hyperwhisper.com/en/user/sign-in?returnTo=/%09/evil.com\n```\n\n**consequence** — 307 to `https://evil.com/` from the product's own domain. Chains with Finding 1: a phishing page on `evil.com` that clones the HyperWhisper dashboard is reached via a hyperwhisper.com link. Note also that the credits deep-link puts `licenseKey=` in the sign-in page URL, so a variant that leaks the key to the third-party origin is one hop away.\n\n**Asymmetry worth naming:** this same value at EP-6 is **not** exploitable, because better-auth's global `originCheckMiddleware` validates `body.callbackURL` against `/^\\/(?!\\/|\\\\|%2f|%5c)[\\w\\-.\\+/@]*(?:\\?[\\w\\-.\\+/=&%@]*)?$/` (`auth/trusted-origins.mjs:14`), which rejects tabs. So `sanitizeLicenseKeyRedirect` at `auth-license-key-plugin.ts:87` is dead weight (strictly weaker than the check already in front of it), and the one call site where the repo's sanitizer is the *sole* gate — `middleware.ts:135` — is exactly where it fails. **The repo's own sanitizer is only load-bearing where it is broken.**\n\n**confidence** — high (bypass verified by running the exact implementation)\n\n---\n\n## Finding 3 — The credential-shape seam: a query-string bearer token is also a session-minting credential\n\nThe same 79-bit string (`lib/services/license-key.ts:29`, `HW-` + 16 chars of a 31-symbol alphabet) plays three roles that were designed independently:\n\n**(a) URL-borne bearer token to the cloud service.** `hyperwhisper-cloud/src/routes/transcribe.ts:393` — comment says *\"Auth (query params only)\"*. Also `hyperwhisper-cloud/src/routes/usage.ts:103` (`c.req.query('license_key')`) and `hyperwhisper-cloud/src/routes/ws-streaming-deepgram.ts:146-147` (`url.searchParams.get('license_key')`) — so it is in the **WebSocket URL** too. Cloud code is careful (`maskLicenseKey` at `middleware/auth.ts:31,107`) but the *URL itself* is logged by every proxy, CDN and platform request log in the path.\n\n**(b) Plaintext in response bodies and web URLs.** `app/api/customer/profile/route.ts:46` returns `key: license.key` unredacted. `app/api/license/credits/route.ts:33` takes it as a `GET` query param → full URL in access logs. `app/[locale]/credits/page.tsx` puts it in the address bar and in browser history. Emails print it in full (`lib/templates/license-email.ts:35,46,88,94`, `credit-mint-email.ts:42,52,89,95`).\n\n**(c) Full session-minting credential.** `auth-license-key-plugin.ts:63-74`, no second factor, no email confirmation, no device binding.\n\n**seam** — the cloud service and the license-validate APIs were written under *\"this is an API key for a metered service; worst case someone burns the customer's credits\"*. `auth-license-key-plugin.ts` upgraded it to *\"this is a password\"* without changing any of the places that treat it as low-value. Nothing was tightened; the assumption on the *other* side of every one of those call sites was silently invalidated.\n\n**consequence** — anyone who reads a cloud access log, a Vercel request log line, a proxy log, a support screenshot, or the browser history of a shared machine gets: full web session (7 days), the customer's email and Stripe customer id (`profile/route.ts:47-49`), all their other license keys in plaintext, and the ability to spend their credits. The victim has no way to invalidate it (see Finding 5) and no session list UI exists.\n\n**confidence** — high (mechanism certain; per-deployment log retention determines exploitability)\n\n---\n\n## Finding 4 — The 5/60s rate limit on the session-minting endpoint is memory-backed, so on serverless it is per-instance\n\n**trace**\n1. `auth-license-key-plugin.ts:88-96` — plugin `rateLimit` `{ window: 60, max: 5 }` on `/sign-in/license-key`\n2. `src/lib/auth.ts:9-36` — **no `rateLimit` block**, no `secondaryStorage`\n3. `node_modules/better-auth/dist/context/create-context.mjs:163-168` — `storage: options.rateLimit?.storage || (options.secondaryStorage ? \"secondary-storage\" : \"memory\")` → **`\"memory\"`**; `enabled: options.rateLimit?.enabled ?? isProduction`; global default `window: 10, max: 100`\n4. `node_modules/better-auth/dist/api/rate-limiter/index.mjs:7,235-239` — a plain module-level `Map`\n\n**seam** — `auth-license-key-plugin.ts:88` ↔ `auth.ts:9`. The plugin author assumed the rate-limit counter is shared and durable; `auth.ts` never configured storage, and the drizzle adapter is present but `rateLimit.storage: \"database\"` was not selected. On Vercel/Fly each lambda instance has its own `Map`, and it dies on cold start. The advertised 5/60s is really 5-per-60s-per-warm-instance.\n\nCompounding it, two unauthenticated key-validity **oracles** exist, both un-throttled (they are plain Next route handlers, not under `/api/auth/`, so better-auth's limiter never sees them):\n- `app/api/license/credits/route.ts:33-56` — `GET ?license_key=` returns `400 \"License key not found\"` vs `400 \"License is revoked\"` vs `200 {credits}`\n- `app/api/checkout/credits/route.ts:88-108` — same three-way distinction\n\nAn attacker enumerates against those, then redeems a hit at EP-6 in a single request that never trips the limit.\n\n**consequence** — 79 bits still makes blind guessing infeasible, so this is not \"accounts fall over tonight\". What it does mean is that the *only* stated defence for a password-equivalent endpoint does not exist in production, so any partial key disclosure (a truncated log — note `license/credits/route.ts:128` logs `license_key.substring(0, 7)`, and `checkout/credits/route.ts:90-92` logs the same 7 chars — a shoulder-surf, a partly-redacted screenshot) becomes directly brute-forceable. `substring(0,7)` is `HW-XXXX`, i.e. it discloses the first segment, cutting the search space by ~20 bits.\n\n**confidence** — medium-high\n\n---\n\n## Finding 5 — Revoking a license does not revoke sessions minted from it\n\n**trace**\n1. `nextjs/server/api/routers/admin/customers.ts:289-341` — `refund` mutation; `:337-339` `if (revokeLicense) await updateAccountKey(licenseKeyId, { status: \"revoked\" })`\n2. No `deleteSessions` / `revokeSessions` call anywhere in `customers.ts` or `src/lib/db-layer.ts` (grepped)\n3. `auth-license-key-plugin.ts:63` — the session row references `user.id`, **not** the license; `src/db/schema/auth.ts:16-27` has no license column on `session`\n\n**seam** — `auth-license-key-plugin.ts:63` ↔ `admin/customers.ts:338`. The plugin's `createSession(foundUser.id)` deliberately drops the license from the session's identity (correct in isolation — credits are pooled per user, `profile/route.ts:38-41`). The refund path assumes that flipping `accountKeys.status` is the revocation. Neither is wrong alone; together, revocation is a no-op for the web session.\n\n**trigger_sketch** — customer signs in at `/en/user/sign-in` with `HW-…`; admin runs `admin.customers.refund({ licenseKeyId, revokeLicense: true })`; customer reloads `/en/user/dashboard`.\n\n**consequence** — for up to 7 days after a refund+revoke, the refunded customer keeps a working web session: they can read `/api/customer/profile`, see their remaining pooled balance, and hit `/api/checkout/credits`… which *does* correctly reject the revoked key (`checkout/credits/route.ts:102-107`), so the failure is a confusing half-state rather than free credits. The sharper case is the compromise scenario: **a user whose key leaked has no way to cut off the attacker's session** — rotating/revoking the key is the only lever the product offers and it does not touch `session`.\n\n**confidence** — high\n\n---\n\n## Finding 6 — `/sign-in/magic-link` is an unauthenticated \"send email to arbitrary address\" from the product's domain\n\n**trace**\n1. `src/lib/auth.ts:23-32` — `magicLink({ sendMagicLink })` → `resend.emails.send({ from: DEFAULT_FROM_EMAIL, to: email, ... })`\n2. `src/lib/auth.ts:9-36` — no `rateLimit`, and **no plugin-scoped rate limit** for the magic-link paths (contrast `auth-license-key-plugin.ts:88-96`, which has one)\n3. `create-context.mjs:163-168` — falls back to the global default `window: 10, max: 100`, memory-backed (same defect as Finding 4)\n\n**seam** — `auth-license-key-plugin.ts:88` ↔ `auth.ts:23`. Whoever added the license-key plugin recognised that an unauthenticated auth endpoint needs throttling and wrote a rule scoped by `pathMatcher(path) => path === \"/sign-in/license-key\"`. That rule's existence makes the *absence* of any equivalent for magic-link read as deliberate when it is almost certainly just untouched — the magic-link plugin predates it and nobody revisited it.\n\n**trigger_sketch**\n```\nPOST /api/auth/sign-in/magic-link\nContent-Type: application/json\n{\"email\":\"victim@third-party.example\"}\n```\nrepeated. No cookie → `validateOrigin` returns early at `origin-check.mjs:108` (`if (!(forceValidate || useCookies)) return`), so no `Origin` header is even needed. ~100 per 10s per warm instance, and the counter is per-instance.\n\n**consequence** — (a) email-bomb any third party from `DEFAULT_FROM_EMAIL`, torching the Resend sender reputation and getting the domain blocklisted, which breaks the *license delivery* emails that are the product's only key-delivery channel; (b) Resend spend; (c) account enumeration is *not* possible here (better-auth's magic-link sends regardless), which is the one saving grace.\n\n**confidence** — medium-high\n\n---\n\n## Finding 7 — Unescaped `${url}` in the magic-link email is currently saved only by a library regex the repo never references\n\n**trace**\n1. `src/lib/auth.ts:62` — `<a href=\"${url}\" ...>` inside `magicLinkEmailHtml`\n2. `nextjs/lib/templates/escape-html.ts:12-17` — `escapeHtml` exists and is used by `license-email.ts`, `welcome-email.ts`, `credit-mint-email.ts`, `credit-topup-email.ts` — **every template except this one**, which lives in `auth.ts` rather than `lib/templates/`\n3. `url` = `${baseURL}/magic-link/verify?token=…&callbackURL=${callbackURL}`; `callbackURL` is attacker-supplied in the POST body\n4. `api/index.mjs:157-160` + `origin-check.mjs:53-71` — global middleware runs `validateURL(callbackURL, \"callbackURL\")`\n5. `auth/trusted-origins.mjs:14` — `allowRelativePaths` regex `^\\/(?!\\/|\\\\|%2f|%5c)[\\w\\-.\\+/@]*(?:\\?[\\w\\-.\\+/=&%@]*)?$`\n\n**seam** — `escape-html.ts` ↔ `auth.ts:38-62`. The escaping convention was established, documented (\"*Always escape user-derived values before embedding them in HTML*\", `escape-html.ts:8-9`) and applied to four templates; the fifth template was written in a different file and missed the convention. It is currently *not* exploitable because the path charset `[\\w\\-.\\+/@]` excludes `\"`, `<`, `>` and `%`, and the query charset excludes `\"` and `<`. But that is an upstream library detail with no test in this repo pinning it.\n\n**trigger_sketch** — no working payload today. It becomes live the moment any of: better-auth relaxes that regex, `advanced.disableOriginCheck`/`skipCSRFCheck` is set, `/sign-in/magic-link` is added to `disabledPaths`-style skip arrays, or a second caller of `magicLinkEmailHtml` appears.\n\n**consequence** if it lands: arbitrary HTML into an email that (i) is sent from the product's authenticated sending domain and (ii) goes to **an address the attacker names** — i.e. self-service phishing-with-your-own-DKIM, targeting anyone.\n\n**confidence** — medium (as a latent seam), low (as an exploitable bug today). Reported because the mitigation is entirely accidental.\n\n---\n\n## Finding 8 — Error-contract mismatch: EP-6 returns `{error}`, its own middleware returns `{message, code}`\n\n**trace**\n1. `auth-license-key-plugin.ts:30,41,55,68` — every failure returns `ctx.json({ error: \"…\" }, { status: 4xx })`\n2. `sign-in/page.tsx:66-69` — `if (!res.ok) setLicenseError(data.error ?? \"Sign-in failed. Please try again.\")`\n3. `origin-check.mjs:64` — a rejected `callbackURL` throws `APIError.from(\"FORBIDDEN\", BASE_ERROR_CODES.INVALID_CALLBACK_URL)`, whose serialized body is `{ code, message }` — **no `error` field**\n\n**seam** — `auth-license-key-plugin.ts` ↔ `origin-check.mjs`. The plugin defined a private error shape; the framework layer wrapped around it emits a different one. The client only knows the plugin's shape.\n\n**trigger_sketch** — any `returnTo` the repo's sanitizer accepts but better-auth's regex rejects. `%` in the *path* is one: a victim bookmarked/was redirected from a path with a percent-escape, e.g. arriving at\n```\n/en/user/sign-in?licenseKey=HW-…&returnTo=/en/user/dashboard%2Ftab\n```\n`sanitizeReturnTo` passes it (`middleware.ts` also happily sets `returnTo` from a raw `request.nextUrl.pathname` at `:92` and `:120`, which preserves percent-encoding). EP-6 then 403s before the handler ever runs.\n\n**consequence** — the user is shown a generic **\"Sign-in failed. Please try again.\"** with a valid license key. Retrying is deterministic-fail because the bad `returnTo` is still in the URL. The real reason (`INVALID_CALLBACK_URL`) is only in the server log. Unfalsifiable-looking support ticket.\n\n**confidence** — medium\n\n---\n\n## Finding 9 — Already-signed-in users silently lose the `licenseKey` deep-link, topping up the wrong wallet\n\n**trace**\n1. `credits/page.tsx:28-31` — deep-link carries `licenseKey` *and* `returnTo`\n2. `middleware.ts:130-137` — `if (isUserSignIn) { if (hasSessionCookie(request)) { … redirect to sanitizeReturnTo(returnTo, …) } }` — **the `licenseKey` param is dropped and the page never mounts**\n3. `sign-in/page.tsx:49-54` — the only consumer of `licenseKey` never runs\n\n**seam** — `credits/page.tsx:30` ↔ `middleware.ts:131`. The credits page's comment (`credits/page.tsx:13-16`) explicitly states the intent: *\"Native-app deep links still carry a key and route through sign-in so the user lands on their dashboard to top up an existing wallet.\"* The middleware's assumption is that the sign-in page has nothing to do for an authenticated visitor. Both are locally reasonable; combined, the deep-link's payload is discarded exactly when the user already has *some* session.\n\n**trigger_sketch** — user is signed in as key A (or, per Finding 1, as the attacker). Desktop app opens\n```\nhttps://hyperwhisper.com/en/credits?license_key=HW-BBBB-…\n```\n→ middleware sends them straight to `/en/user/dashboard` for the **A** account.\n\n**consequence** — the user buys credits believing they are topping up key B; the checkout resolves against whatever the dashboard is showing. Multi-key customers (and anyone hit by Finding 1) pay into the wrong wallet with no error at any step.\n\n**confidence** — medium\n\n---\n\n## Finding 10 — Middleware's admin check trusts a `Host`-derived origin for its own session fetch\n\n**trace**\n1. `middleware.ts:50-52` — `const baseUrl = request.nextUrl.origin; fetch(\\`${baseUrl}/api/auth/get-session\\`, { cookie: … })`\n2. `middleware.ts:98-104` — `if (user.role !== \"admin\") redirect(dashboard)`\n\n**seam** — `middleware.ts:51` ↔ `middleware.ts:99`. `request.nextUrl.origin` is derived from the `Host`/`X-Forwarded-Host` header. A request with a spoofed host makes the middleware fetch its role oracle from an attacker-controlled server, which can answer `{\"user\":{\"role\":\"admin\"}}`.\n\n**consequence** — bounded: the page itself re-checks server-side (`app/[locale]/user/(authenticated)/customers/page.tsx:37`, `devices/page.tsx:32`) and `adminProcedure` re-checks (`server/api/trpc.ts:50,148`), so this only gets past the *routing* gate. Real defence-in-depth erosion, not a takeover. Vercel normally rejects unrecognised hosts.\n\n**confidence** — low-medium\n\n---\n\n## Verified negatives (checked, no bug found)\n\n- **`role: input: false` is genuinely the only guard, and it holds.** Grepped every reference to `role` in `nextjs/src`, `app`, `lib`, `components`, `server`. The complete set of *writers* is: the column default `text(\"role\").default(\"user\")` (`src/db/schema/auth.ts:13`) and the better-auth `additionalFields` default (`src/lib/auth.ts:14-20`). **No repo code ever assigns `user.role`** — not `getOrCreateUser` (`db-layer.ts:932-960`, which inserts only `id/email/name/emailVerified`), not the Stripe webhook, not the internal grant route. Readers are `trpc.ts:50`, `customers/page.tsx:37`, `devices/page.tsx:32`, `(authenticated)/layout.tsx:42`, `dashboard/page.tsx:35`, `middleware.ts:99`. `emailAndPassword` is off, so `/sign-up/email` is not mounted at all; `/update-user` strips `input: false` fields. Escalation to `admin` requires direct DB write. Note as a *fragility*, not a bug: EP-6 bypasses better-auth's field layer entirely (`db.select().from(user)` at `auth-license-key-plugin.ts:47-51`, raw row passed to `setSessionCookie` at `:74`), so the session carries whatever `role` is in the row — fine today, but it means the `input: false` guard is not in this code path.\n- **`ctx.json(body, { status })` does propagate the status.** `better-call@1.3.6/dist/context.mjs:71-78` stores `routerResponse`; `to-response.mjs:123` reads `routerResponse?.status`. `body: routerResponse?.body || json` correctly falls through to `json` since `{status:400}` has no `.body`. So `res.ok` is `false` on the client as intended. (One caveat: `context.mjs:72` — `if (!context.asResponse) return json` — means a *server-side* `auth.api.signInLicenseKey()` call would receive `{error: \"…\"}` as a **success** value with the 400 lost. No such call site exists in the repo today.)\n- **Session `ipAddress`/`userAgent` are recorded despite the bare `createSession(foundUser.id)` call.** The signature is `(userId, dontRememberMe, override, overrideAll)` (`db/internal-adapter.mjs:162`) — there is no request-context parameter to forget — and the implementation pulls headers itself from `getCurrentAuthContext()` async-local storage (`:163-166`). So `src/db/schema/auth.ts:22-23` gets populated. The manual header reads at `auth-license-key-plugin.ts:77-82` are redundant with, not a substitute for, that.\n- **Unencoded `params.locale` at `credits/page.tsx:30` is not injectable.** `middleware.ts:9-13` builds `LOCALE_REGEX` from `src/i18n/locales.ts`, and `routing.ts` uses `localePrefix: \"always\"`, so next-intl rewrites any non-locale first segment. `params.locale` is always one of the allow-listed values, none of which contain `&`, `#`, `\\` or `/`.\n- **`/%2f%2fevil.com` is not a `sanitizeReturnTo` bypass** — `new URL()` does not decode `%2f` in path position; it stays same-origin. (Confirmed by execution.)\n\n---\n\n## depth_limits\n\n- Did **not** run the test suite (`bun test tests`) — I read `tests/license-key-redirect.test.ts` in full and reproduced the sanitizer logic standalone in Node instead, which is what Finding 2 needed. No test was modified.\n- Did **not** verify Finding 2's client-side leg (`router.push(data.redirect)` at `sign-in/page.tsx:71`) against Next's actual router internals — the *middleware* leg (`middleware.ts:135`, explicit `new URL`) is confirmed, so the finding stands regardless. `router.push` is likely also affected but I am not asserting it.\n- Did **not** confirm production values of `BETTER_AUTH_URL`, `NODE_ENV`, or whether Vercel/Fly is the deploy target — Finding 4's severity (memory storage → per-instance) assumes a multi-instance serverless deploy. On a single long-lived Node process the 5/60s limit works as written.\n- Did **not** read the Stripe webhook (`app/api/webhooks/stripe/route.ts`) or `src/lib/license-validation.ts` end-to-end — out of the T10 path; touched only where key normalisation mattered.\n- Did **not** enumerate `hyperwhisper-cloud` log sinks (`fly-log-shipper/`) to establish log retention, which is what turns Finding 3 from a mechanism into a measured exposure.\n- Case-sensitivity check on the key was partial: `findAccountByKey` (`db-layer.ts:158`) uses `eq(accountKeys.key, key.trim())`, case-**sensitive**, while `getAccountKeysByEmail` (`:188`) and `getOrCreateUser` (`:938`) lowercase their input. Since `generateLicenseKey` only emits uppercase and every read path uses `.trim()` without case folding, this is consistent — but I did not check whether any import/migration path inserted a lowercased key.\n- `licenseKey: z.string()` with no `.min()`/`.max()`: an empty string reaches `findAccountByKey(\"\")` and simply misses (no row has an empty key); a multi-megabyte string is passed to an indexed `eq` with no length cap — a weak DoS vector, not developed further. The raw `user-agent` interpolation at `auth-license-key-plugin.ts:81` is a log-quoting nit only (Node rejects CR/LF in header values).\n\n## files_opened\n\nRepo:\n-
